		<title>Accelerating Plug-And-Play Prototyping In Chiplet-Based Designs</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/#respond</comments>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Shared resources, a mix of tools running at different speeds, and multiple dies are adding new challenges for design teams.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/">Accelerating Plug-And-Play Prototyping In Chiplet-Based Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Verifying chips is becoming much more difficult in multi-die assemblies. Unlike in the past, when simulation was sufficient, designs now require a combination of simulation, emulation, and FPGA prototyping to ensure that entire systems will work as expected. Purna Mohanty, CEO of SignatureIP, talks about the impact of chiplets and shared memory resources, the challenges of version control across multiple flavors of I/O, and the challenges engineering teams face when hardware-accelerated tools are running at different speeds than the actual design.</p>
<p><iframe src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/lkPX3lINOX4" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/">Accelerating Plug-And-Play Prototyping In Chiplet-Based Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Adding Network Security to AI, HPC and Data Centers</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/#respond</comments>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How data center operators can protect critical data and communications without compromising the performance demanded by distributed AI and HPC workloads.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/">Adding Network Security to AI, HPC and Data Centers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>AI and HPC workloads are transforming data center networking by creating distinct front-end and back-end network domains, each with different performance, scalability, and operational requirements. While front-end networks connect users, applications, storage, and management systems, back-end networks provide the high-speed connectivity required among GPUs, accelerators, and compute nodes. This white paper examines the architectural and security considerations associated with these specialized networks and explains how data center operators can protect critical data and communications without compromising the performance demanded by distributed AI and HPC workloads.</p>
<p>Learn:</p>
<ul>
<li>How front-end and back-end networks serve different roles in modern AI and HPC infrastructure</li>
<li>The performance, operational, and security requirements of each network domain</li>
<li>How to protect data and communications across increasingly distributed compute environments</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Read <a href="https://go.rambus.com/thank-you-adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers">more here</a>.</strong></p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Why automotive-grade PQC IP matters, what sets it apart from general-purpose PQC, and what SoC design teams should look for when choosing a PQC accelerator.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/built-to-last-why-automotive-grade-pqc-cant-wait/">Built to Last: Why Automotive-Grade PQC Can’t Wait</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Vehicles entering production today may remain in service for decades, while the cryptographic landscape continues to evolve. As quantum computing advances, automotive SoC designers need to prepare now for a transition from today’s public-key cryptography to quantum-resistant security. This white paper explores what makes post-quantum cryptography (PQC) different for automotive applications and the key considerations for building secure, adaptable platforms designed for long operational lifetimes.</p>
<p><b>What you will learn</b></p>
<ul>
<li>Why automotive PQC needs to start now</li>
<li>How hybrid cryptography enables the PQC transition</li>
<li>What makes automotive-grade PQC different</li>
<li>What to look for in a PQC accelerator</li>
<li>How Synopsys Agile PQC PKA supports secure, adaptable designs</li>
</ul>
<p>Read <a href="https://www.synopsys.com/resources/agile-pqc-pka.html">more here</a>.</p>

		<title>Validate Sovereign AI Sites Before You Commit Capital: eBook</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/validate-sovereign-ai-sites-before-you-commit-capital-ebook/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/validate-sovereign-ai-sites-before-you-commit-capital-ebook/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Cadence]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 08:03:39 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>

		<category><![CDATA[Cadence Reality Digital Twin Platform]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[cooling]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How to validate sites before commitment, protect capacity after deployment, and plan for long-term performance.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/validate-sovereign-ai-sites-before-you-commit-capital-ebook/">Validate Sovereign AI Sites Before You Commit Capital: eBook</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Sovereign AI raises the stakes on every infrastructure decision. When data residency, operational control, and compliance requirements limit where workloads can run, there’s far less room for error. A site that looks viable on paper can become a costly problem if cooling, airflow, or resiliency assumptions don’t hold up under real operating conditions.</p>
<p>That’s why early validation matters. The Cadence Reality Digital Twin Platform helps teams create high-fidelity digital representations of AI infrastructure to evaluate power, cooling, thermal performance, site conditions, and operating scenarios before deployment. In one case, a data hall appeared sound in review, but once leakage and a cooling failure were modeled, more than half the cabinets ran too hot. The design itself hadn’t changed, only the assumptions behind it. Inside, you’ll see how teams can uncover issues like these early, before they turn into expensive constraints.</p>
<p>Download the eBook to discover:</p>
<ul>
<li>Why sovereign AI makes infrastructure decisions harder to reverse and more expensive to get wrong</li>
<li>How to validate site readiness under real operating conditions, not just ideal design assumptions</li>
<li>How sovereign constraints can expose hidden performance gaps in airflow, cooling, and facility operations</li>
<li>What it takes to support dense AI workloads when relocation and fallback options are limited</li>
<li>How to plan for infrastructure drift, hardware refresh cycles, and rising density demands</li>
<li>A working checklist for what to validate before commitment, during design, and after deployment</li>
</ul>
<p>When fallback paths are limited, real-world validation matters more. This eBook shows how physics-based validation helps teams make better siting decisions, avoid preventable retrofits, and sustain performance over time.</p>
<p>Read the <a href="https://www5.cadence.com/sovereign-ai-site-validation.html?utm_source=SE&amp;utm_medium=article&amp;utm_campaign=DataCenter_Sovereign_AI_ebook">ebook here</a>.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24283578" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=760%2C256&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="760" height="256" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=300%2C101&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=1024%2C344&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=768%2C258&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=1536%2C516&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?resize=600%2C202&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-1.28.57-PM.png?w=1988&amp;ssl=1 1988w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 760px) 100vw, 760px" /></p>

		<title>Beyond The Chip: How AI Is Reshaping Semiconductor Design And Manufacturing</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/beyond-the-chip-how-ai-is-reshaping-semiconductor-design-and-manufacturing/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/beyond-the-chip-how-ai-is-reshaping-semiconductor-design-and-manufacturing/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Prashanth Aprameyan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 08:01:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - MD]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundry Focus]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Manufacturing, Packaging and Materials]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[advanced packaging]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[backside power delivery]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[design-technology co-optimization]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DTCO]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GAA FETs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[gate all around]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intel]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[machine learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ML]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[scaling]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[semiconductor manufacturing]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283684</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Power delivery, connectivity, advanced packaging, and supply chain resilience are emerging as the primary constraints on AI scaling. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/beyond-the-chip-how-ai-is-reshaping-semiconductor-design-and-manufacturing/">Beyond The Chip: How AI Is Reshaping Semiconductor Design And Manufacturing</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>While AI is transforming nearly every industry, one of its most significant impacts is happening behind the scenes, inside the semiconductor factories responsible for building the chips that power today&#8217;s AI revolution.</p>
<p>During a recent presentation at the Global Semiconductor Alliance (GSA) European Executive Forum, I discussed how the conversation around AI scale is evolving. For years, the semiconductor industry focused primarily on transistor scaling. Today, however, the biggest challenges facing AI designers extend far beyond the transistor. Power delivery, connectivity, advanced packaging, and supply chain resilience are emerging as the primary constraints on AI scaling.</p>
<p>At Intel Foundry, we&#8217;re addressing these challenges on two fronts. First, we&#8217;re developing advanced process and packaging technologies that help customers build increasingly capable AI systems. Second, we&#8217;re using AI and machine learning (ML) throughout our technology development and manufacturing operations to improve quality, accelerate yield learning, and help customers bring products to market faster.</p>
<h2>Three forces shaping the future of semiconductor design</h2>
<p>Three major AI trends are shaping what modern silicon must deliver:</p>
<p><strong>Agentic AI</strong> introduces multistep reasoning workloads that are far less predictable than traditional computing. Autonomous agents place new demands on power delivery and high-performance connectivity, making efficient scaling increasingly important.</p>
<p><strong>Physical AI</strong> brings intelligence to edge devices and real-world systems. Scalable AI-enabled automation and modeling are essential for cost-effective, high-yield manufacturing. The foundry industry increasingly views both agentic AI and physical AI as significant long-term growth drivers.</p>
<p><strong>Sovereign AI</strong> initiatives are increasing demand for secure, resilient, and geographically diverse manufacturing and supply chains to support both business continuity and national security needs. By maintaining Intel Foundry’s production capabilities across North America, Europe, and Asia, customers gain flexibility in determining where their products are manufactured while improving supply chain resilience and security.</p>
<h2>Multi-dimensional scaling: The new path forward</h2>
<p>A decade ago, the semiconductor industry largely focused on the system-on-chip (SoC) model, where a single chip integrated as much functionality as possible. Today, innovation has shifted toward a system of chips, using chiplet architectures that combine multiple specialized dies, memory stacks, and accelerators into a single solution. This approach provides greater modularity, scalability, and faster innovation cycles while enabling the performance required for advanced AI workloads.</p>
<p>At the same time, scaling is no longer simply a matter of shrinking transistors. The industry is increasingly focused on optimizing performance across the entire system, from compute and memory to packaging and interconnects. Advanced process technologies such as gate-all-around (GAA) transistors, including Intel 18A with RibbonFET GAA technology, provide improved electrostatic control and support scaling beyond traditional FinFET architectures.</p>
<p>Power delivery also has been reimagined. Intel 18A uses PowerVia, our backside power delivery technology that routes power through the back of the wafer rather than sharing frontside interconnect layers with signal routing. By separating power and signal paths, PowerVia reduces resistance, voltage drop, and power delivery noise. Combined with high-density metal-insulator-metal (MIM) capacitors, the architecture improves power integrity. PowerVia delivers up to a 10% increase in cell utilization¹ and a 4% improvement in ISO-power performance.² Connection to the transistor layer is enabled through innovative nano through-silicon vias (TSVs) that are approximately 500 times smaller than conventional TSVs.</p>
<p>While backside power adds manufacturing complexity, Intel Foundry optimized the overall cost structure by maintaining 32 nm lower-metal pitches on the frontside, enabling direct-print patterning rather than more expensive multi-patterning approaches. This balanced architecture allows Intel 18A and Intel 18A-P to deliver improved performance-per-watt and more efficient power delivery for AI-era workloads while remaining cost competitive. Intel 18A with PowerVia can provide approximately 18% performance-per-watt improvement versus Intel 3.³ Now in risk production, Intel 18A-P is expected to deliver an additional 9% performance-per-watt improvement over Intel 18A⁴ (see the <a href="https://www.intel.com/content/dam/www/central-libraries/us/en/documents/2026-06/foundry-18a-technology-brief.pdf">Intel 18A family technical brief</a> for details).</p>
<p>As systems become increasingly modular, chips must communicate efficiently with one another. This is where Universal Chiplet Interconnect Express (UCIe) plays a critical role. UCIe provides a standardized interconnect for high-bandwidth, low-latency communication between chiplets, simplifying integration and enabling more scalable heterogeneous systems.</p>
<p>Advanced packaging serves as the glue that connects all these technologies together. A complete AI package may combine I/O dies, advanced logic silicon, HBM, 2.5D integration, and 3D integration. Intel Foundry supports these designs through technologies including Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge-T (EMIB-T), Foveros, and Foveros Direct.</p>
<p>EMIB-T incorporates power-transfer capabilities directly into the bridge structure, helping improve power delivery efficiency and reduce inductive power droop in multi-die AI systems. It can also reduce package material requirements by eliminating the need for a full silicon interposer in many applications.</p>
<p>Foveros Direct uses hybrid bonding to enable 3D integration, allowing designers to stack leading-edge compute dies on optimized base dies. This approach helps customers balance performance, power efficiency, and total cost of ownership (TCO) by placing each function on the process technology best suited to its requirements.</p>
<h2>Using AI across the semiconductor manufacturing lifecycle</h2>
<p>While AI is creating new challenges for chip designers, it is also helping solve many of the challenges associated with manufacturing advanced semiconductors.</p>
<p>At Intel Foundry, AI and ML are already deeply integrated into our manufacturing workflows, actively supporting how we develop and manufacture leading-edge process technologies (see Figure 1). One area where AI is delivering value is design technology co-optimization (DTCO). Advanced process nodes involve enormous design spaces with countless tradeoffs between power, performance, and area (PPA). AI helps engineers explore these possibilities more efficiently and identify optimal solutions.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283685" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=1912%2C921&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1912" height="921" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?w=1912&amp;ssl=1 1912w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=300%2C145&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=1024%2C493&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=768%2C370&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=1536%2C740&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Intel_Beyond-the-Chip-AI-Reshaping-Design-Mfg-fig1.webp?resize=600%2C289&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Intel Foundry’s deployment of AI and ML efforts across the manufacturing flow.</strong></p>
<p>AI&#8217;s role extends beyond product design and into every stage of the manufacturing lifecycle, transforming how we identify and correct manufacturing issues. Our factories collect nearly a billion images each week, generating petabytes of manufacturing data. ML systems analyze these images to detect defects, classify failure patterns, identify yield risks, and improve design rules. This enables designers targeting Intel 18A to identify and address potential layout issues earlier in the design process, reducing risk before designs reach production.</p>
<p>Beyond defect detection, we use ML techniques including neural networks and ensemble decision trees to isolate root causes and accelerate defect resolution. The result is faster learning cycles and more predictable yield progression as new technologies ramp into production.</p>
<p>We also use digital twin technologies and predictive maintenance systems to monitor manufacturing equipment continuously. By identifying potential tool issues before they disrupt production, these systems help maintain the high levels of uptime required in semiconductor manufacturing operations that run around the clock.</p>
<h2>Turning manufacturing innovation into customer value</h2>
<p>In AI system design, success depends on optimizing the entire platform rather than simply choosing the most advanced transistor technology. By using AI throughout the manufacturing lifecycle, Intel Foundry is helping customers bring the next generation of AI systems to market faster and more efficiently.</p>
<h3>Endnotes</h3>
<ol>

		<title>Choosing The Right Power Transistor: Why Application Fit Matters More Than Technology</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/choosing-the-right-power-transistor-why-application-fit-matters-more-than-technology/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/choosing-the-right-power-transistor-why-application-fit-matters-more-than-technology/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Infineon]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 08:01:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Whitepapers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GaN]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GaN HEMTs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IGBT]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IGBTs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Infineon]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[MOSFETs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[power devices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[power electronics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[power transistors]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[SiC]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[SiC MOSFETs]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283588</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Silicon MOSFETs, IGBTs, SiC MOSFETs, and GaN HEMTs each offer meaningful advantages, but those advantages do not carry the same value across every converter, motor drive, or power stage.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/choosing-the-right-power-transistor-why-application-fit-matters-more-than-technology/">Choosing The Right Power Transistor: Why Application Fit Matters More Than Technology</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Selecting a power transistor has become less about identifying a universally superior technology and more about matching device behavior to the demands of the application. Silicon MOSFETs, IGBTs, SiC MOSFETs, and GaN HEMTs each offer meaningful advantages, but those advantages do not carry the same value across every converter, motor drive, or power stage. The decisive factors are the electrical and thermal realities of the design: bus voltage, current profile, switching frequency, allowable temperature rise, control strategy, commutation behavior, PCB parasitics, and system cost targets.</p>
<p>The discussion begins with the switching and conduction mechanisms that shape the behavior of every power transistor, including crossover loss, inductive switching, parasitic capacitance, reverse conduction and charge-recovery behavior, and the practical distinction between hard-switched and soft-switched operation. These operating modes create different selection priorities: hard-switched stages are often strongly influenced by turn-on loss and commutation behavior, while soft-switched stages depend on the charge to be commutated, the available current, and the ability to maintain soft switching across the operating range. On that basis, it compares silicon MOSFETs, IGBTs, SiC MOSFETs, and GaN HEMTs in terms that matter to designers working on real hardware: voltage range, current capability, switching performance, gate-drive implications, thermal behavior, and implementation sensitivity.</p>
<p>The discussion then moves into application context, including PFC, resonant conversion, synchronous rectification, and motor-drive design decisions, where lower device loss alone does not guarantee the best overall result. Thermal margins, board design, control choices, and total system cost all influence whether a given device technology improves the converter in a meaningful way. The application examples also show when direct transistor substitution can address the dominant limitation and when a topology change or hybrid implementation offers a more effective path. The role of simulation is also considered, showing where modeling is useful for relative comparison and where hardware validation remains essential before a design decision is finalized.</p>
<p>The result is a practical framework for choosing among silicon, IGBT, SiC, and GaN technologies based on application fit rather than technology fashion, enabling power-electronics designers to select the device, and where appropriate the topology, that best aligns with the converter’s actual operating conditions and design priorities.</p>
<p>Read <a href="https://www.infineon.com/gated/infineon-switching-characteristics-comparison-whitepaper-en-1-clean_d4012540-f6b7-4961-b30d-3b868d4f7a09">more here</a>.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/choosing-the-right-power-transistor-why-application-fit-matters-more-than-technology/">Choosing The Right Power Transistor: Why Application Fit Matters More Than Technology</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Chiplets for physical AI; rack-level considerations; verification complexity; sparse matrix workloads; AI data centers.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/blog-review-nov-4-3/">Blog Review: Nov. 4</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Cadence&#8217;s Mick Posner introduces a <a href="https://community.cadence.com/cadence_blogs_8/b/ip/posts/when-ai-moves-the-world-building-a-chiplet-standard-for-physical-ai">physical AI sub-project</a> workstream to extend the OCP&#8217;s Foundation Chiplet System Architecture&#8217;s data center, cloud, and server-centric baseline with the specification requirements to satisfy real-time, safety-critical physical systems, while retaining everything FCSA already defines.</p>
<p>Synopsys&#8217; Charlie Matar considers how design focus is shifting from the <a href="https://www.synopsys.com/blogs/chip-design/rack-scale-ai-factory-power-cooling.html">server to the rack</a>, with silicon behavior shaping rack-level power and cooling requirements, while rack constraints influence decisions inside chips and packages.</p>
<p>Siemens&#8217; Harry Foster warns that while <a href="https://blogs.sw.siemens.com/verificationhorizons/2026/10/28/are-we-looking-beyond-the-numbers-in-the-2026-functional-verification-study/">verification complexity</a> has been managed by dividing large problems into smaller pieces, new challenges are emerging as the number and diversity of interactions influencing correct SoC behavior grows.</p>
<p>Arm&#8217;s Zenon (Zhilong) Xiu examines how <a href="https://developer.arm.com/community/arm-community-blogs/b/architectures-and-processors-blog/posts/part-5-sme2-quarter-tile-and-structured-sparsity-outer-product-instructions">quarter-tile and structured sparsity</a> outer product instructions make the SME2 programming model better suited to small, dense, quantized, and sparse matrix workloads in modern AI software.</p>
<p>Keysight&#8217;s Linas Dauksa digs into <a href="https://www.keysight.com/blogs/en/tech/educ/ai-data-center-infrastructure">AI data center</a> infrastructure, including key components, energy demands and power density challenges, the array of liquid cooling technologies available, and what differentiates hyperscale facilities.</p>
<p>SEMI&#8217;s Sungho Yoon considers factors related to a forecasted increase in <a href="https://www.semi.org/en/blogs/what-is-driving-silicon-wafer-demand-recovery">wafer shipments</a>, including rising process material sales and a high number of new and upcoming production lines.</p>
<p>Plus, check out the blogs featured in the latest <a href="https://semiengineering.com/newsletter/systems-design-oct-2026/">Systems &amp; Design newsletter</a>:</p>
<p>Technology editor Brian Bailey poses the age-old question for anyone involved in the verification of semiconductor systems: in the age of AI, <a href="https://semiengineering.com/putting-ai-to-the-test/">who verifies the verifier</a>?</p>
<p>Axiomise&#8217;s Vidya Chandran Darbari and Ashish Darbari dig into what <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-complexity-between-the-bits/">biological reasoning</a> can teach us about verifying silicon systems.</p>
<p>Keysight&#8217;s Chris Mueth explains why applying the appropriate level of <a href="https://semiengineering.com/is-it-getting-hot-in-here-why-thermal-is-no-longer-a-signoff-problem/">simulation and abstraction</a> at each stage of the design process is needed for effective thermal analysis.</p>
<p>Siemens EDA&#8217;s Emma-Jane Crozier explores how <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physics-based-validation-the-foundation-of-trustworthy-agentic-eda/">self-verifying agents</a> validate their decisions and why physics-based engines are the authority on correctness.</p>
<p>Arteris&#8217; Leon Brown looks at which products may fall within the <a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-eu-cyber-resilience-act-reaches-chip-and-ip-suppliers/">EU Cyber Resilience Act&#8217;s</a> scope and what information may need to be provided to customers.</p>
<p>Movellus&#8217; Aakash Jani and Siemens&#8217; Henrique Mendes and Robert Wilcox separate false failures caused by <a href="https://semiengineering.com/from-functional-monitoring-to-in-system-test-extending-voltage-aware-observability/">voltage droop</a> from real structural defects.</p>
<p>ChipAgents&#8217; Zackary Glazewski demonstrates how to deal with <a href="https://semiengineering.com/scaling-autonomous-root-cause-analysis-for-regression-triage/">difficult bugs</a> while intelligently deciding what to investigate, what to rerun, and where to spend compute.</p>
<p>Vinci&#8217;s Hardik Kabaria examines why <a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-physics-needs-to-move-at-the-pace-of-design/">physics-based analysis</a> becomes more consequential as chips, packages, boards, and systems are designed together.</p>
<p>Cadence&#8217;s Reela Samuel breaks down how to orchestrate <a href="https://semiengineering.com/what-can-ai-agents-actually-do-for-me/">multi-step engineering</a> workflows to reduce repetitive effort and explore more design options.</p>
<p>Synopsys&#8217; Anand Thiruvengadam outlines how <a href="https://semiengineering.com/long-horizon-agents-autonomous-engineering-from-silicon-to-systems/">autonomous engineering agents</a> pursue an objective requiring hundreds or thousands of reasoning steps while maintaining context, evaluating results, and adapting along the way.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/blog-review-nov-4-3/">Blog Review: Nov. 4</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

		<title>Neural Super Resolution</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/neural-super-resolution/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/neural-super-resolution/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Imagination Technologies]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 08:01:01 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Whitepapers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[compute shader]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[converged acceleration]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data locality]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[deterministic graphics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GPU]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GPU neural graphics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Imagination Technologies]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[microtiling]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Neural Super Resolution]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[NSR]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[real-time graphics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[self-compression]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283585</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Insight into how neural graphics can improve image quality and performance while simplifying deployment on power- and bandwidth-constrained devices.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/neural-super-resolution/">Neural Super Resolution</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As AI becomes an increasingly important part of the graphics pipeline, Neural Super Resolution (NSR) offers a new approach to generating higher-quality images without the cost of rendering every pixel natively. This white paper explores how Imagination’s NSR technology uses neural reconstruction to upscale lower-resolution frames into higher-resolution outputs, combining current-frame data, temporal history and learned inference to deliver high visual quality with reduced bandwidth, power and memory demands. Unlike approaches that rely on separate AI accelerators, NSR runs directly within the GPU, enabling graphics, compute and AI workloads to work together in a unified execution environment.</p>
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Readers will learn the principles behind neural super resolution, the role of Converged Acceleration in modern GPU design, and how Imagination’s E-Series architecture integrates matrix acceleration and neural processing into the graphics pipeline. The paper also explains the importance of data locality, microtiling and Self-Compression in achieving efficient neural rendering, while examining the performance, bandwidth and energy benefits of GPU-native super resolution. For graphics architects, developers and SoC designers, it provides insight into how neural graphics can improve image quality and performance while simplifying deployment on power- and bandwidth-constrained devices.</p>
<p>Read <a href="https://www.imaginationtech.com/resources/neural-super-resolution/">more here</a>.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone  wp-image-24283586" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=552%2C241&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="552" height="241" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=300%2C131&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=1024%2C446&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=768%2C335&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=1536%2C670&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?resize=600%2C262&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-02-at-2.01.43-PM.png?w=2014&amp;ssl=1 2014w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 552px) 100vw, 552px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig.1: Projected Doom Eternal performance and bandwidth improvements using NSR. Projected estimates for Doom Eternal compare native 4K rendering with 1080p rendering reconstructed to 4K using NSR on an EXD-64-2048 MC1 GPU modelled at 1 GHz. These figures should be treated as workload- and configuration-specific projections. Source: Imagination.</strong></p>

		<item>
		<title>Randomized Caches in HW: Overheads And Limitations (RUB, Max Planck, Georgia Tech)</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/randomized-caches-in-hw-overheads-and-limitations-rub-max-planck-georgia-tech/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/randomized-caches-in-hw-overheads-and-limitations-rub-max-planck-georgia-tech/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Technical Paper Link]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 03 Nov 2026 18:01:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Architectures]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical Papers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ASIC]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[cache security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[FPGA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Georgia Tech]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[hardware security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Last-Level Cache]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Max Planck Institute for Security and Privacy]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[microarchitecture]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[processor architecture]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[randomized caches]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[RISC-V]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Ruhr University Bochum]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[side channel attacks]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283637</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at Ruhr University Bochum, the Max Planck Institute for Security and Privacy, and Georgia Tech published “Randomized Caches in Hardware: Challenges and Trade-offs.” Abstract Excerpt: &#8220;Randomized caches protect against side-channel attacks by obfuscating the mapping of memory addresses to cache locations. Existing proposals typically describe architectural behavior, leaving practical hardware implementation challenges largely unexplored.... <a class="read_more" href="https://semiengineering.com/randomized-caches-in-hw-overheads-and-limitations-rub-max-planck-georgia-tech/">&#187; read more</a></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/randomized-caches-in-hw-overheads-and-limitations-rub-max-planck-georgia-tech/">Randomized Caches in HW: Overheads And Limitations (RUB, Max Planck, Georgia Tech)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Researchers at <strong>Ruhr University Bochum</strong>,<strong> the Max Planck Institute for Security and Privacy</strong>, and <strong>Georgia Tech</strong> published “Randomized Caches in Hardware: Challenges and Trade-offs.”</p>
<p><strong>Abstract Excerpt</strong>:<br />
&#8220;Randomized caches protect against side-channel attacks by obfuscating the mapping of memory addresses to cache locations. Existing proposals typically describe architectural behavior, leaving practical hardware implementation challenges largely unexplored. We implement four prominent designs, CEASER-S, ScatterCache, SassCache, and MIRAGE, and integrate them as the last-level cache (LLC) of a multi-core out-of-order RISC-V system-on-chip (SoC). We identify common and design-specific implementation challenges and develop practical solutions. We evaluate complete FPGA implementations by running SPEC CPU 2017 rate and GAPBS workloads to completion, providing, to our knowledge, the first comparative evaluation of these designs on a common full-system hardware platform.&#8221;</p>
<p>Find the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00282">technical paper here</a>. October 2026</p>
<p>Wang, Xinrui, Pascal Kosak, Christof Paar, Daniel Genkin, Yuval Yarom, and Anirban Chakraborty. &#8220;Randomized Caches in Hardware: Challenges and Trade-offs.&#8221; arXiv preprint arXiv:2611.00282 (2026). CC BY 4.0 l<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/">icense</a>.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/randomized-caches-in-hw-overheads-and-limitations-rub-max-planck-georgia-tech/">Randomized Caches in HW: Overheads And Limitations (RUB, Max Planck, Georgia Tech)</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
